we will lift Markovian families on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and show that the lifted family is associated to the same class of geometric types as its projection on
\mathcal{P}
(see Proposition
7.5
). Next, in Section
7.2
we will explain how to use rectangle paths in order to navigate simultaneously into two bifoliated planes up to surgeries endowed with Markovian families associated to the same class of geometric types (see Theorem
7.7
). The main goal of this section is to use Markovian families as coordinate systems of the bifoliated plane up to surgeries
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and show that Markovian families that are associated to the same class of geometric types correspond to two compatible coordinate systems in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
(see Theorem
7.9
). The proof of this result is rather technical, but also the most important step in the proof of Theorem B.

7.1.
Markovian families in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}

Let
M
be a closed, oriented 3-manifold carrying a transitive Anosov flow
\Phi
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the bifoliated plane of
\Phi
, carrying a Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
. Denote by
\Gamma
the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}
, by
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\Phi
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
and by
\overline{\pi}
the projection of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathcal{P}
. Using as a starting point our definition of Markovian family in
\mathcal{P}
, we can extend the notion of Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. This allows us to define rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and use them later as coordinate systems for comparing bifoliated planes up to surgeries.

We define rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
in the exact same way as for
\mathcal{P}
(see Definition
3.1
). We then define a Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
as follows:

Definition 7.1
.

A
Markovian family of rectangles
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is a set of mutually distinct rectangles
(R_{i})_{i\in I}
covering
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
such that

(1)

(R_{i})_{i\in I}
is the union of a finite number of orbits of rectangles by the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma)

(2)

(\overline{\pi}(R_{i}))_{i\in I}
is a Markovian family of
\mathcal{P}

Remark 7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a Markovian family in
\mathcal{P}
. The lift
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
of
\mathcal{R}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is also a Markovian family.

Indeed, all points in
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{P}
do not belong in the interior of some rectangle in
\mathcal{R}
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is a family of rectangles in the sense of Definition
3.1
, that covers
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and that satisfies the second property of Definition
7.1
. The family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
consists of a finite number of
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma)
-orbits of rectangles as a result of Remark
6.6
. We deduce that
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is a Markovian family in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

The definitions of the notions
k
-th successor/predecessor and
s
-crossing predecessor can be extended for Markovian families on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
:

Definition 7.3
.

For any two rectangles
R_{1},R_{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
, we will say that
R_{1}
is a
predecessor
(resp.
successor
) of
R_{2}
if
\overset{\circ}{R_{1}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{2}}\neq\emptyset
and the
\overline{\pi}(R_{1})\subset\mathcal{P}
is a predecessor (resp. successor) of
\overline{\pi}(R_{2})
.

We similarly define
predecessors/successors of generation
k\geq 2
and
s
-crossing predecessors/successors
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

We define a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
in the same way as in
\mathcal{P}
(see Definition
5.1
).

Definition 7.4
.

A point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called
periodic
(resp.
boundary arc
,
boundary periodic
) if its projection on
\mathcal{P}
is a periodic (resp. boundary arc, boundary periodic) point.

A stable/unstable leaf
f
of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
will be called
periodic
if there exists
g\in\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma)
such that
g.f=f

We also define a
polygonal/closed polygonal/good polygonal curve
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
in the same way as in
\mathcal{P}
(see Definitions
5.2
and
5.4
).

By the same arguments as in Section
3.2
, we can show that the family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
is canonically associated to a unique class of geometric types. In fact, we have the following result:

Proposition 7.5
.

The Markovian families
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{R}
are associated to the same class of geometric types

Proof.

Let us endow the foliations
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}
with an orientation. This orientation canonically defines an orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
. Consider also a choice of representatives
r_{1},...,r_{n}
of every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
and a lift
\overline{r_{i}}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
of every
r_{i}
. Thanks to Remark
6.6
, the rectangles
\overline{r_{i}}
are representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. According to Remark
3.18
, together with this choice of representatives and orientations, the Markovian families
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{R}
can be associated to a unique geometric type
\overline{G}=(\overline{n},(\overline{h_{i}})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(\overline{v_{i}})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\overline{\mathcal{H}},\overline{\mathcal{V}},\overline{\phi},\overline{u})
and
G=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
respectfully.

Let us now go back to the construction of
G
and
\overline{G}
(see proof of Theorem
3.17
). The number
n
(resp.
\overline{n}
) in
G
(resp.
\overline{G}
) is equal to the number of distinct orbits in
\mathcal{R}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
) by the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
(resp.
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma)
) . By Remark
6.6
, we get that
n=\overline{n}
.

Next, the number
h_{i}
(resp.
v_{i}
) will correspond to the number of successors (resp. predecessors) of
r_{i}
. Same for
\overline{h_{i}}
and
\overline{v_{i}}
. By Definition
7.3
, we easily have that
\overline{r_{i}}
and
r_{i}
have the same number of predecessors and successors. In other words,
h_{i}=\overline{h}_{i}
and
v_{i}=\overline{v}_{i}
.

Now, let us order the successors (resp. predecessors) of every
r_{i}
from bottom to top (resp. from left to right) using the orientations of
\mathcal{F}^{u}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}^{s}
). We will denote the
k
-th successor (resp.predecessor) of
r_{i}
for this order by
H_{i}^{k}
(resp.
V_{i}^{k}
). Recall that
\mathcal{H}=\{H^{k}_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V^{k}_{i},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
. We similarly define
\overline{V_{i}^{k}}
,
\overline{H^{k}_{i}}
for
\overline{r_{i}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{H}},\overline{\mathcal{V}}
. By our choice of orientations on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
we have that
\overline{\pi}(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})=H_{i}^{k}
and
\overline{\pi}(\overline{V_{i}^{k}})=V_{i}^{k}
. Therefore, we can canonically identify
\mathcal{H}
with
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
and
\mathcal{V}
with
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
.

Also,
\phi(H^{k}_{i})=V^{l}_{j}
if and only if there exists
g\in\pi_{1}(M)
such that
g(H^{k}_{i})=r_{j}
and
g(r_{i})
is the
l
-th predecessor (from left to right) of
r_{j}
. Recall that the previous
g
is unique if it exists and that
u(H^{k}_{i})=+1
if
g
preserves the orientations of the stable/unstable foliations and
u(H^{k}_{i})=-1
if not. The functions
\overline{\phi}
and
\overline{u}
are similarly defined. Thanks to Remark
6.6
, we deduce that
\phi(H^{k}_{i})=V^{l}_{j}
if and only if
\overline{\phi}(\overline{H^{k}_{i}})=\overline{V^{l}_{j}}
. Finally, take
\overline{h}\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}
,
h=\overline{\pi}(\overline{h})\in\mathcal{H}
,
g
(resp.
\overline{g}
) the unique element in
\pi_{1}(M)
(resp.
\pi_{1}(M-\Gamma)
) such that
g(h)=r_{j}
(resp.
\overline{g}(\overline{h})=\overline{r_{j}}
). It is not difficult to see that
g
preserves the orientation of the foliations if and only if
\overline{g}
does. Hence,
u(h)=\overline{u}(\overline{h})
. Finally, using the identifications between
\mathcal{H}
and
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
,
\mathcal{V}
and
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
, we have that
\phi
and
\overline{\phi}
(resp.
u
and
\overline{u}
) define the same functions from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{V}
, which proves the desired result.
∎

7.2.
Rectangle paths as coordinate systems

Let
(M_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
be two transitive Anosov flows,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
their bifoliated planes,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{u}
the stable and unstable foliations in
\mathcal{P}_{1,2}
. Assume that
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
carry two Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
associated canonically by Theorem
3.17
to the same class of geometric types. By appropriately choosing representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, and orientations for the foliations
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{u}
, we may assume that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are canonically associated to the same geometric type
(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
, thanks to Remark
3.18
and Lemma
3.19
.

Let us denote
\Gamma_{1,2}
(resp.
\Gamma_{1,2}^{M_{1,2}}
) the boundary periodic orbits associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1,2}
(resp. in
M_{1,2}
),
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{1,2}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1,2}}
their stable and unstable singular foliations,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1,2}}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
and
\overline{\Gamma_{1,2}}
the lifts of
\Gamma_{1,2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
. We would like to show that any rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
corresponds to a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

By Proposition
7.5
, since
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are associated to the same geometric type, we can endow
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1,2}}
with orientations and we can chose representatives in every rectangle orbit in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1,2}}
so that
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
are associated to the same geometric type
(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
. By the proof of Proposition
7.5
, every
\pi_{1}(M_{1,2}-\Gamma_{1,2}^{M_{1,2}})
-orbit of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1,2}}
corresponds to a unique
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and a unique couple
(h_{i},v_{i})
of the geometric type.

Definition 7.6
.

We will say that
R^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
(resp.
R^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
) are of
type
i
if the
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-(\Gamma_{1})_{M_{1}})
-orbit (resp.
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-(\Gamma_{2})_{M_{2}})
) of
R^{1}
(resp.
R^{2}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
) corresponds to the same integer
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and couple
(h_{i},v_{i})
of the geometric type.

Fix
r_{0}^{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
r_{0}^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
a rectangle of the same type as
r_{0}^{1}
. From now on, we will call
r_{0}^{1,2}
the
origin rectangle
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
and we will also call any rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
starting from
r_{0}^{1,2}
a
centered rectangle path
.

Theorem 7.7
.

Any centered rectangle path
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
corresponds canonically to a unique centered rectangle path
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket

r^{1}_{i}
and
r^{2}_{i}
are of the same type.

Proof.

Take
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
.

Let us start by constructing
r^{2}_{1}
. Since
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
is a rectangle path
r^{1}_{1}
is a predecessor or a successor of
r^{1}_{0}
. Assume without any loss of generality that it is a successor. Using the orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{1}}
, assume that
r^{1}_{1}
is the
k-
th successor of
r^{1}_{0}
from bottom to top. Take
r^{2}_{1}
to be the
k
-th successor of
r^{2}_{0}
from bottom to top (we use here the orientation on
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}_{2}}
). By the proof of Proposition
7.5
and the fact that
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
correspond to the same geometric type, we have that
r^{2}_{1}
is of the same type as
r^{1}_{1}
. We construct in the exact same way
r^{2}_{2},...,r_{n}^{2}
by induction.
∎

We will call the rectangle path
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
the
associated rectangle path
of
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
. In the next pages, we will use rectangle paths and associated rectangle paths as coordinate systems that will allow us to compare
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. The main goal of this section is to prove that those coordinate systems are compatible:

Theorem 7.8
.

Any two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
ending by the same rectangle are associated to two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
ending by the same rectangle.

The previous theorem is equivalent to the following:

Theorem 7.9
.

Any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
is associated to a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Proof of the equivalence of theorems
7.8
and
7.9
.

(
7.8

\Rightarrow

7.9
): Take
r^{1}_{0}
the trivial rectangle path and
R
any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Since
R
is closed, it also ends at
r^{1}_{0}
. By Theorem
7.8
, the two rectangle paths are associated to two centered rectangle paths of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
ending at the same rectangle. But the trivial rectangle path
r^{1}_{0}
is associated by Theorem
7.7
to the trivial rectangle path
r^{2}_{0}
. Therefore,
R
is associated to a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

(
7.9
\Rightarrow

7.8
):
Take two centered rectangle paths
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{n}
and
r^{1}_{0}=R^{1}_{0},R^{1}_{1},...,R^{1}_{m}=r^{1}_{n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. The rectangle path
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{n},R^{1}_{m-1},R^{1}_{m-2},...,R^{1}_{1},r^{1}_{0}
is a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Therefore, by Theorem
7.9
it is associated to a closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, say
r^{2}_{0},...r^{2}_{n},R^{2}_{m-1},R^{2}_{m-2},...,R^{2}_{1},r^{2}_{0}
. It is not difficult to see, that
r^{2}_{0},...r^{2}_{n}
is the associated rectangle path of
r^{1}_{0},...r^{1}_{n}
and that
r^{2}_{0}=R^{2}_{0},R^{2}_{1},...,R^{2}_{m-1},R^{2}_{m}:=r^{2}_{n}
is the associated rectangle path of
R^{1}_{0},R^{1}_{1},...,R^{1}_{m}
. We deduce that the two associated rectangle paths end by the same rectangle.

∎

Our goal from now on will be to prove Theorem
7.9
, which is the most important step in the proof of Theorem B. In order to do that we will introduce the notion of
homotopy of rectangle paths
, we will show that only closed rectangle paths can be homotopic to the trivial rectangle path (see Section
7.3
), that any closed centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path
r_{0}^{1}
(see Section
7.4
) and finally that any homotopy of rectangle paths on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
is associated to a homotopy of rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
(see Section
7.5
).

7.3.
Different types of homotopies of rectangle paths

Take
(M,\Phi)
a transitive Anosov flow,
\mathcal{P}
its bifoliated plane,
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}
the stable/unstable foliations in
\mathcal{P}
,
\mathcal{R}
a Markovian family on
\mathcal{P}
,
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{P}
the set of boundary points of
\mathcal{R}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
the bifoliated plane of
\Phi
up to surgeries on
\Gamma
,
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and
\overline{\Gamma}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}
and
\Gamma
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
the singular stable/unstable foliations in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
.

By repeating the arguments used in Section
5.1
, we can prove the analogues of Lemma
5.5
and Propositions
5.6
and
5.7
for
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. It therefore remains true that we can associate good polygonal curves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
to rectangle paths of
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and vice versa. In the following lines, we will use this association in order to define the notion of homotopy for rectangle paths.

We will fix for the rest of this section
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
.

Definition 7.10
.

We will say that two rectangle paths are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
if they are equal.

Two good polygonal curves
\gamma,\delta:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
associated to the rectangle path
R_{0},...,R_{n}
will be called
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
(relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
).

The notion of homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
between two polygonal curves
\gamma
and
\delta
associated to the same rectangle path can be described in terms of the existence of a homotopy
H:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
from
\gamma
to
\delta
satisfying the following property: for every
t\in[0,1]
there exists a
(n+2)
-uple
d_{0}=0<d_{1}<...<d_{n+1}=1
such that
H(t,[d_{k},d_{k+1}])\subset R_{k}
. It is possible to prove that the two definitions coincide. The same applies for homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
that will later be defined. For the sake of simplicity, we will define homotopies of good polygonal curves in terms of the associated rectangle paths, rather than the existence of a homotopy satisfying a number of properties.

Notice that the notion of homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
for good polygonal curves depends on the rectangle path relatively to which we perform it. Indeed, take
\gamma
a good polygonal curve contained inside
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
and also in the union of two predecessors of
R
,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
such that
\gamma(0)\in R_{1}
and
\gamma(1)\in R_{2}
. By Proposition
5.6
, we can associate
\gamma
to a non-trivial rectangle path starting from
R_{1}
and also to the trivial rectangle path
R
. Notice that relatively to
R
the curve
\gamma
is homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
to a point. Relatively to the non-trivial rectangle path starting from
R_{1}
, this is not the case.

Convention 7.11
.

By an abuse of language, whenever the context makes it clear, we will omit the rectangle paths relatively to which we will perform homotopies of good polygonal curves.

Figure 11
.
\gamma
and
\delta
are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}

Definition 7.12
.

Consider
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path such that there exists
k\in\llbracket 0,n-2\rrbracket
for which
R_{k}=R_{k+2}
. We are going to say that the rectangle paths
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},R_{k+4},...
and
R_{0},...,R_{n}
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
.

Consider
\gamma
,
\delta
two good polygonal curves associated to the rectangle paths
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},R_{k+4},...,R_{n}
respectively (see Figure
11
). We will say that
\gamma
and
\delta
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
(relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R_{0},..,R_{k},R_{k+3},R_{k+4},...,R_{n}
).

It is not difficult to see that both homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
or type
\mathcal{B}
(seen as maps
H:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
) can be performed in the interior of rectangles without ever crossing a boundary arc point. We would like at this point to define a homotopy allowing good polygonal curves to cross boundary arc points in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. We will name this homotopy,
homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
. In order to introduce the notion of homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
, we will first need to introduce the notion of
cycle
around a boundary arc point:

Definition 7.13
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point and
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and two germs of quadrants of
p
. Assume without any loss of generality that
p\in\partial^{s}L_{0}
(see Figure
12
). Consider
r
the stable boundary component of
L_{0}
containing
p
. By Remark
4.7
, there exists
L_{1}
an
r
-crossing predecessor of
L
such that
p\in\partial{L_{0}}\cap\partial{L_{1}}
.

Consider
r_{1}
the unstable boundary component of
L_{1}
containing
p
. Again, by Remark
4.7
, there exists
L_{2}
an
r_{1}
-crossing successor of
L_{1}
such that
p\in\partial{L_{2}}\cap\partial{L_{1}}
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}=\emptyset
. By the same exact procedure, we construct
L_{3}
and
L_{4}
such that
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}=\emptyset
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}=\emptyset
. We are going to call
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
a
cycle
around
p
starting from
L_{0}
.

Figure 12
.

Remark 7.14
.

For any boundary arc point
p
, there exists a cycle around
p
.

Indeed, by Definition
7.13
, it suffices to show that there exists
L\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. Take
L\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing
p
. Since
p
is a boundary arc point, it is contained in the boundary of
L
. If furthermore
p
is not a corner point of
L
, then
L
contains the germs of two quadrants of
p
. If
p
is a corner point, let us name
s
the stable boundary of
L
in which it is contained. Consider
L^{\prime}
the
s
-crossing predecessor of
L
that contains
p
. The rectangle
L^{\prime}
has the desired property.

Boundary arc points, by definition, are not periodic points, they are therefore regular points of the foliations
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}
. Take
p
a boundary arc point and
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
a cycle around of
p
. Let us denote
G_{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}
a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
p
, where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{-,+\}
. By the definition of a cycle, up to cyclic permutation of the indices of the
L_{i}
a cycle verifies one of the two following statements:

Positive cycle

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

Negative cycle

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(-,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(+,+)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset

•

\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(+,-)}\neq\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap G_{(-,-)}\neq\emptyset

In the first case, we will call the cycle
positive
and in the second one
negative
. Notice in particular, that for every cycle
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
around
p
, we have that
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}\neq\emptyset
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point and
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. There exist exactly two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
: one positive and one negative.

Proof.

Without any loss of generality, we can assume that
p
is contained in a stable boundary component of
L_{0}
, say
s
, and that a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
is contained in
L_{0}
. By Remark
4.7
, since
p
is a boundary arc point and any two distinct
s
-crossing predecessors of
L_{0}
intersect only along their boundaries, there exist exactly two
s
-crossing predecessors
L_{1},L_{1}^{\prime}
of
L_{0}
containing
p
:
L_{1}
contains a germ of the
(+,+)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
and
L_{1}^{\prime}
a germ of the
(-,+)
and
(-,-)
quadrants of
p
. Since
L_{1}
is an
s
-crossing predecessor, the point
p
is contained in the interior of some unstable boundary component of
L_{1}
. Therefore, by our previous argument the rectangle
L_{2}
of Definition
7.13
is uniquely defined. Similarly, the rectangles
L_{3}
and
L_{4}
of Definition
7.13
are uniquely defined. The same argument applies for
L_{1}^{\prime}
. We thus obtain exactly two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
: one positive and one negative.
∎

Lemma 7.16
.

Take
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point. If
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle around
p
, then
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
is also a cycle around
p
.

Proof.

Let us assume without any loss of generality that
p\in\partial^{s}L_{0}
. Denote by
s
the stable boundary component of
L_{0}
containing
p
. Let us show that
L_{3}
is an
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
.

If
s^{\prime}
is the stable boundary component of
L_{2}
containing
p
, then by construction
L_{3}
is a
s^{\prime}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{2}
. By Remark
4.7
, the unstable boundary of
L_{3}
intersects
s^{\prime}
along boundary arc points. By the Markovian intersection property,
L_{3}\cap L_{0}
is a vertical subrectangle of
L_{0}
(see Figure
12
). Since
L_{3}\cap s
consists of two boundary arc points, we deduce from Remark
4.7
that
L_{3}
is a
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
. We show in the exact same way, that
L_{2}
is a crossing successor of
L_{3}
, that
L_{1}
is a crossing predecessor of
L_{2}
and that
L_{4}
is a crossing successor of
L_{1}
. Finally, since
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
is a cycle, we have that
\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{0}}=\emptyset
,
\overset{\circ}{L_{3}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{1}}=\emptyset
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{4}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}}=\emptyset
. We conclude that
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
is also a cycle around
p
.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

•

Notice that in the above lemma, the rectangles
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}
are canonically associated to
p
independently of the choice of
L_{0}
. Indeed, assume that
L_{1}
contains the point
p
and a germ of the
(+,+)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p
. Then
L_{1}
corresponds to the unique rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
containing in its unstable boundary
p
and the boundary arc point after
p
in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)}
.

•

Generally,
L_{0}\neq L_{4}
. This results from the fact that, contrary to the case of successors/predecessors, if
L_{1}
is a
s
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
, then
L_{0}
is not necessarily a
u
-crossing successor of
L_{1}
(see Figure
12
).

Definition 7.18
.

Consider
L_{0},...,L_{n}
a sequence of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that for every
i
, the rectangle
L_{i+1}
is a successor or predecessor or
u
-successor or
s
-predecessor of
L_{i}
. We will call
L_{0},...,L_{n}
a
generalised rectangle path
. By Proposition
3.15
and Lemma
3.12
, for every
i
there exist a unique monotonous rectangle path (see Definition
5.1
)
L_{i}=L_{i0},L_{i1},...L_{i(s(i)+1)}=L_{i+1}
going from
L_{i}
to
L_{i+1}
.
We are going to say that the generalised rectangle path
L_{0},...,L_{n}
is
associated
to the rectangle path
L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s(0)},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s(1)},L_{2},....,L_{n-1},L_{(n-1)1},...,L_{(n-1)s(n-1)},L_{n}
.

Definition 7.19
.

Consider
R_{0},...,R_{n}
a rectangle path,
p\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
a boundary arc point,
L_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}}
such that
L_{0}
contains
p
and the germs of two quadrants of
p
. Denote by
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
and
(L_{0},L_{3},L_{2},L_{1},L_{4})
the two cycles around
p
starting from
L_{0}
. Consider
(L_{0},L_{1}...,L_{k})
the first
k+1
terms of the first cycle with
k\in\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
and
(L_{0},L_{3}...,L_{k})
the part of the second cycle starting from
L_{0}
and ending at
L_{k}
.

Following the notations of Definition
7.18
, we can associate
L_{0},L_{1},...,L_{k}
and
L_{0},L_{3},...,L_{k}
to the rectangle paths
L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s(0)},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s(1)},...,L_{k}
and

L_{0},L^{\prime}_{01},...,L^{\prime}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},L_{3},L^{\prime}_{31},...,L^{\prime}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,L_{k}
respectively. Assume that the first rectangle path is contained in
R_{0},...,R_{n}
. In other words, assume that
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is of the form

R_{0},...,R_{m},L_{0},L_{01},...,L_{0s(0)},L_{1},L_{11},...,L_{1s(1)},...,L_{k},R_{l},R_{l+1},...,R_{n}

Consider now the rectangle path

R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}:=R_{0},...,R_{m},L_{0},L^{\prime}_{01},...,L^{\prime}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},L_{3},L^{\prime}_{31},...,L^{\prime}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,L_{k},R_{l},R_{l+1},...,R_{n}

We are going to say that the rectangle paths
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
are
homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
.

Take
\gamma
and
\delta
two good polygonal curves associated to the rectangle paths
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
respectively (see Figure
13
). We will say that
\gamma
is homotopic to
\delta
by a
homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
(relatively to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
R^{\prime}_{0},...,R^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}
).

Figure 13
.

Definition 7.20
.

Take
R:=r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{n}
and
R^{\prime}:=r_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{m}
two centered rectangle paths. We are going to say that
R
and
R^{\prime}
are
homotopic
if there exists
R_{0}:=R,R_{1},...,R_{s}:=R^{\prime}
a sequence of rectangle paths such that
R_{i}
is homotopic to
R_{i+1}
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
(relatively to
R_{i}
and
R_{i+1}
).

Let us remark that because of our definition of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, a closed rectangle path is only homotopic to closed rectangle paths. Therefore, a rectangle path homotopic to the trivial rectangle path, is necessarily closed. It turns out that the converse of the above result is also true:

Theorem 7.21
.

Any centered closed rectangle path
r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{n}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path
r_{0}

The above theorem constitutes the key argument in the proof of Theorem
7.9
, which we will later show that it implies Theorem B.

7.4.
Proof of Theorem
7.21

The proof of the above theorem is rather technical and will be split two parts:

(1)

Proving Theorem
7.21
in the case of rectangle paths that can be associated to simple closed curves:

Proposition 7.22
.

Let
\gamma
be a simple closed and good polygonal curve associated to the rectangle path
R_{0},..,R_{n}
.

•

If
R_{0}=R_{n}
, then
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path.

•

If
R_{n}
is a successor or predecessor of generation
k
of
R_{0}
and
R_{0}=r_{0},r_{1},..r_{k}=R_{n}
is the unique monotonous rectangle path from
R_{0}
to
R_{n}
, then
R_{0},...,R_{n}
is homotopic to
r_{0},...,r_{k}
.

(2)

Proposition
7.22
implies the general case

Proof of Proposition
7.22

We will prove Proposition
7.22
by induction.

Let
\gamma
be a good closed polygonal curve associated to
R_{0},...,R_{n}
and
M
be the number of boundary arc points in the interior of
\gamma
(i.e. the closure of the bounded connected component of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}-\gamma
).

Notice here that
M<\infty
. Indeed, by compactedness, we can cover the interior of
\gamma
by a finite number of rectangles of our Markovian family
\overline{\mathcal{R}}
. By Lemmas
4.4
,
4.5
and Remark
4.7
, the number of boundary arc points in the interior of
\gamma
is finite except if a point of
\overline{\Gamma}
is in the interior of
\gamma
. This is impossible, since
\gamma
is good and no loop in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
can go around a point in
\overline{\Gamma}
.

Let
n
be the length of the good polygonal curve
\gamma
. We will apply an induction on the well ordered set of couples
(M,n)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
endowed with the lexicographic order:
(M_{1},n_{1})<(M_{2},n_{2})
if and only if
M_{1}<M_{2}
or
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
n_{1}<n_{2}
. At every step of our induction, we will perform a finite sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
on
\gamma
-therefore also on its associated rectangle path-, that will produce a new simple, good and closed polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
such that
(M(\gamma^{\prime}),n(\gamma^{\prime}))<(M(\gamma),n(\gamma))

Notice that any simple and good polygonal curve has length at least equal to 4. Hence, the smallest such curves have the form of a rectangle. We will therefore initialize our induction by considering the case where
\gamma
is a rectangle containing no boundary arc points in its interior.

Let us first fix some notations. By Remark
5.8
, there exists
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}=1
a
(n+2)
-uple such that
\gamma(c_{i},c_{i+1})\subset R_{i}
and a function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\{R_{0},...,R_{n}\}
associated to
c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}
sending points of
\gamma
to rectangles. Fix such a collection of
c_{i}
. We will assume that we have chosen the
c_{i}
as in Remark
5.9
. Also, by a small abuse of language and notation, for the sake of simplicity if
A
is a connected subset of
\gamma([0,1])
, then we will denote
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(\gamma^{-1}(A))
by
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(A)
and we will refer to it as the rectangle path associated to
A
.

Initializing the induction

Assume that
(M(\gamma),n(\gamma))=(0,4)
. We will show that under this hypothesis
\gamma
is associated to a rectangle path of the desired form.

By Remark
5.3
,
\gamma(0)
is a corner of the rectangle
\gamma
. Let us denote by
u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime}
, following the order in which
\gamma
visits them, the four segments contained in
\gamma
.

We will associate a segment of
\gamma
, say
S
, to
0
if the rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(S)
is trivial and to
1
if not. We can therefore associate
\gamma
to an element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
. Our method for associating rectangle paths to curves (see Proposition
5.6
) restricts the elements of
\{0,1\}^{4}
that can be associated to
\gamma
.

Lemma 7.23
.

If
\gamma
is a rectangle, then the only elements of
\{0,1\}^{4}
that can be associated to
\gamma
are
(0,0,0,0),(0,1,1,1)
,
(0,1,0,0),(1,0,0,0)
and
(1,1,1,1)
. If furthermore, the interior of
\gamma
contains no boundary arc points,
(1,1,1,1)
and
(0,1,1,1)
are impossible.

Proof.

Without any loss of generality, let us assume that
u,u^{\prime}
are unstable segments and
s,s^{\prime}
are stable segments.

If
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(0,0)
, then Remark
5.9
implies that
u^{\prime}
and
s
do not exit
R_{0}
and since
R_{0}
is trivially bifoliated
s^{\prime},u\subset R_{0}
. Therefore, in this case, since
\gamma
is a rectangle,
\gamma
does not exit
R_{0}
at all and is associated to
(0,0,0,0)
.

Suppose now that
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(1,1)
. We can assume without any loss of generality that the (oriented by
\gamma
) segments
u^{\prime}
and
s
are positively oriented (for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
). In this case, Remark
5.9
implies that
u^{\prime}
will exit
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
(see Figure
14
), next
u^{\prime}
will exit
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{2}}
, a
\partial^{s}_{+}R_{u_{1}}
-crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
, and so on, until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime}\cap s)
. Similarly,
s
will exit
R_{u_{N}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{N+1}}
, a
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N}}
. Next,
s
will exit
R_{u_{N+1}}
in order to visit
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N+1}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N+1}}
, and so on until it exits
R_{u_{m-1}}
and visits
R_{m}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u\cap s)
(see Figure
14
).

Notice that
R_{u_{m}}
(resp.
R_{0}
) is a successor of some generation of
R_{u_{N+1}}
(resp.
R_{u_{N-1}}
). Also, since
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
, we have that
R_{u_{N+1}}
and
R_{u_{N-1}}
correspond two distinct successors (of some generation) of
R_{u_{N}}
. Using the previous facts, it is not difficult to see that
\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{m}}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}=\emptyset
. Consequently, if
u
does not exit
R_{u_{m}}
, then the curve
\gamma
cannot be a rectangle. Hence, as before,
u
exits
R_{u_{m}}
in order to visit a
\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{m}}
-crossing predecessor
R_{u_{m+1}}
of
R_{u_{m}}
. After this,
u
exits
R_{u_{m+1}}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{m+1}}
and so on until it exits
R_{u_{k-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{k}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u\cap s^{\prime})
. By the same arguments as before,
\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{k}}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{u_{N}}}=\emptyset
; hence
s^{\prime}
has to exit
R_{u_{k}}
in order to reach
\gamma(0)\in R_{u_{N}}
. We conclude that if
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(1,1)
, then
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is associated to
(1,1,1,1)
.

Figure 14
.

If
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(1,0)
, then as before
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{0}
in order to visit
R_{u_{1}}
, a crossing predecessor of
R_{0}
, next
u^{\prime}
exits
R_{u_{1}}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor of
R_{u_{1}}
and so on until it exits
R_{u_{N-1}}
and visits
R_{u_{N}}:=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime}\cap s)
a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
that contains completely
u^{\prime}
. Since
s
by hypothesis does not exit
R_{N}
and
R_{N}
is trivially bifoliated,
u,s^{\prime}
do not exit
R_{N}
either. Therefore, if
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(1,0)
, then
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is associated to
(1,0,0,0)
.

We show in a similar way that if
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(0,1)
, then
(u^{\prime},s,u,s^{\prime})
is associated to
(0,1,0,0)
or
(0,1,1,1)
.

Finally, let us show that if
\gamma
is associated to an element of
\{0,1\}^{4}
with three consecutive
1
s, then the interior of
\gamma
contains boundary arc points. Assume that
\gamma
is associated to
(1,1,1,1)
(the case
(0,1,1,1)
follows by the same argument). Following our previous notations (see the case
(u^{\prime},s)
associated to
(1,1)
),
u^{\prime}\subset R_{u_{N}}
,
R_{u_{N+1}}
is a
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}
-crossing successor of
R_{u_{N}}
and
s
exits
R_{u_{N}}
. By Remark
5.16
, the domain of
u^{\prime}
and
s
coincide with the interior of
\gamma
, therefore the interior of
\gamma
is trivially bifoliated and contains the
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}\cap\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{N+1}}
(see Figure
14
), which is a boundary arc point according to Remark
4.7
.
∎

We now ready to describe the initialization of our induction. Recall that
\gamma
is associated to the rectangle path
R_{0},...,R_{n}
. Since
\gamma
is a rectangle and contains no boundary arc points in its interior, we have the following 3 cases:

(1)

If
\gamma
corresponds to
(0,0,0,0)
by our proof of Lemma
7.23
, we have that
\gamma
never exits
R_{0}
, it is therefore associated to the trivial rectangle path.

(2)

If
\gamma
corresponds to
(1,0,0,0)
by our proof of Lemma
7.23
(see the case where
(u^{\prime},s)
is associated to
(1,0)
),
\gamma
is associated to a decreasing rectangle path of the form
R_{0},...,R_{u_{N}}
.

(3)

Similarly, to the previous case, if
\gamma
corresponds to
(0,1,0,0)
, then
\gamma
is associated to an increasing rectangle path.

We deduce that if
(M(\gamma),n(\gamma))=(0,4)
, then Proposition
7.22
is true. Let us now describe the step of our induction.

Induction step: the case where
\gamma
is a rectangle with
M(\gamma)>0

Following our previous notations, by our proof of Lemma
7.23
, we have that
\gamma
is associated to either
(1,1,1,1)
or
(0,1,1,1)
. Suppose that
\gamma
is associated to
(1,1,1,1)
. Assume once again that the (oriented by
\gamma
) segments
u^{\prime}
and
s
are positively oriented (for our choice of orientation of
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}}
).

Figure 15
.

During our proof of Lemma
7.23
, we associated to
u^{\prime}
and
s
a generalized rectangle path (see Definition
7.18
)
\mathcal{R}_{u^{\prime},s}:=R_{0},R_{u_{1}},...,R_{u_{N}},R_{u_{N+1}},...,R_{u_{m}}
. Notice that by construction, the rectangle path associated to
\mathcal{R}_{u^{\prime},s}
corresponds to the first part of the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
. Also, we showed that
u^{\prime}\subset R_{u_{N}}
,
s
exits
R_{u_{N}}
in order to visit
R_{u_{N+1}}
and that
\partial^{u}_{+}R_{u_{N}}\cap\partial^{s}_{-}R_{u_{N+1}}
is a boundary arc point contained in the interior of
\gamma
(see Figure
14
). Consider the negative cycle
(R_{u_{N}},R_{u_{N+1}},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4})
around the previous boundary arc point starting from
R_{u_{N}}
. Using the previous cycle, we can deform
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
to a good and simple polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime}
containing strictly less boundary arc points in its interior (see Figure
15
).

We treat the case where
\gamma
is associated to
(0,1,1,1)
in a similar way.

Induction step in the general case

We will assume here that
\gamma
is not a rectangle. In this case, our algorithm begins by choosing one tangency
s
(with respect to the stable or unstable foliation) of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain
D
(see Lemma
5.15
) such that we have the two following conditions:

•

\gamma(0)\notin D
or
\gamma(0)
is a corner point of the rectangle
D
that is not in
s

•

the interior of one side of
\partial D
is disjoint from
\gamma

We will call a tangency with the above properties, a tangency with property
(\star)
. If furthermore,
\gamma(0)\notin D
we will say that the tangency satisfies the strong
(\star)
property.

Lemma 7.24
.

Let
\gamma
be a good, simple and closed polygonal curve of length strictly greater than 4. There exists
s
a tangency of
\gamma
(with the respect to the stable or unstable foliation) with property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Indeed, take
s
to be a stable tangency of
\gamma
of type (a) or (b) (the existence of at least 2 of such tangencies is assured by Lemma
5.13
). Suppose that the domain of
s
, that we will denote by
D
, is not complete. In that case, by Remark
5.16
, there exists a finite number of (stable) segments of
\gamma
in the interior of the stable boundary of
D
that is not
s
. Take
s^{\prime}
one such segment. Since
\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
and
D
is incomplete,
s^{\prime}
is a tangency of type (c) or (d). Therefore, to every tangency of type (a) or (b) with an incomplete domain we can associate at least one tangency of type (c) or (d). We deduce by Lemma
5.13
that there exist at least two tangencies of type (a) or (b) for
\gamma
, whose domains are complete.

Consider now
s
a tangency (with respect to the stable foliation) of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain
D
. The function
\gamma:\mathbb{S}^{1}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
induces a cyclic order on the segments forming
\gamma
. Denote by
u
(resp.
u^{\prime}
) the unstable segment of
\gamma
after (resp. before)
s
(see Figure
17
) and
s^{\prime}
the stable boundary component of
D
that is not
s
. By definition, the domain of
s
satisfies,
\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\gamma=\emptyset
, hence if
\gamma(0)\in D
then
\gamma\in\partial D
.

If
\gamma(0)\in D
, then by Remark
5.3
,
\gamma(0)
cannot belong to the interior of
s
,
u
or
u^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in\partial s
. Since there are at least 2 tangencies (with respect to the stable foliation) of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain, we can assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma(0)\notin s
.

Assume now that
\overset{\circ}{s^{\prime}}
intersects
\gamma
. We can assume without any loss of generality that
u\subset D
(since
D
is complete). Since
\gamma
is good, any two stable segments of
\gamma
do not belong to the same stable leaf of
\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
. Therefore, we have necessarily that the segment of
\gamma
after
u
, say
S
, is contained in
s^{\prime}
. Since
\gamma
is not a rectangle
S\subsetneq s^{\prime}
; we are therefore in the situation of Figure
16
. Hence,
u
is a tangency (with respect to the unstable foliation) of type (a) or (b) with a complete domain. If
\gamma(0)\notin u
, then
u
has property
(\star)
. If
\gamma(0)\in u
, then take
s_{fin}\neq s
to be another stable tangency of
\gamma
with a complete domain (we showed that there are at least 2) and notice that
\gamma(0)\notin s_{fin}
. If
s_{fin}
doesn’t satisfy
(\star)
, then repeat our argument for
s
of this paragraph in order to construct an unstable tangency with propery
(\star)
.
∎

Figure 16
.

Even when
\gamma
is not a rectangle, it is not always possible to find a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. However, there exist cases where this is possible:

Lemma 7.25
.

Let
\gamma
be a simple, closed and good polygonal curve that is not a rectangle. Assume that
\gamma(0)
belongs to a tangency (with respect to the stable or unstable foliation) of type (a) or (b). Then there exists a tangency with strong property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Indeed, assume without any loss of generality that
\gamma(0)\in S
, where
S
is a tangency with respect to the stable foliation of type (a) or (b). By Lemma
5.13
, there exists
s
is a tangency with respect to the stable foliation with of type (a) or (b) such that
\gamma(0)\notin s
. By our proof of Lemma
7.24
, we can assume that
s
has a complete domain
D
. Notice that
D
cannot contain
\gamma(0)
, since a domain of a (stable) tangency of type (a) or (b) cannot intersect a (stable) tangency of type (a) or (b), except when
\gamma
is a rectangle. By our proof Lemma
7.24
, either
s
has strong property
(\star)
and we get the desired result or we are in the case of Figure
16
. In this case, using the notations of Figure
16
, the segment
u
has property
(\star)
and its domain
D^{\prime}\subset D
does not contain
\gamma(0)
.
∎

We will now describe our induction step in the following two subcases:

(1)

there exists
s
a tangency with strong property
(\star)

(2)

no such tangency exists

Induction step in the case where a tangency with strong
(\star)
property exists

Assume without any loss of generality that the stable tangency
s
of
\gamma
has strong property
(\star)
. The function
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}}
endows the segments forming
\gamma
with a total order, for which

is neither the first nor the last segment (since
\gamma(0)\notin s
). Let us denote by

(resp.
U

before (resp. after)
s
and by
s^{\prime}
the stable side of the domain of
s
, say
D
, that is not
s
. We define

and
u:=U\cap D
. We are therefore -up to a change of orientation and up to interchanging
u^{\prime}
and

17
.

Figure 17

As in the previous case, we will associate a segment of
\gamma
, say

, to
0

to an element of
