Before stating the induction step in this case, let us first state a very useful lemma:

Lemma 7.26
.

Let
B:=L_{1},...,L_{k}
be a decreasing rectangle path. Consider any rectangle path of the form
L_{p},w_{B},L_{p^{\prime}}
, where
p,p^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
w_{B}
is a rectangle path formed by rectangles in
B
. If
p^{\prime}>p
(resp.
p>p^{\prime}
,
p=p^{\prime}
), the previous rectangle path is homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
to a decreasing (resp. increasing, trivial) rectangle path.

The above lemma can be easily proven by an induction on the length of
w_{B}
; we will therefore omit its proof.

(a)

Assume that
\gamma_{|[b,1]}
eventually exits
\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor (or successor) of
R_{k}\in A
. By pushing
\gamma
along
D
so that
s
be identified with
s^{\prime}
, we obtain a simple, good and closed curve
\gamma^{\prime}
of strictly smaller length. Let us show that the previous movement corresponds to a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
or
\mathcal{B}
.

As in the case
(2^{\prime})
, our movement pushes the segments
u^{\prime},s,u
of
\gamma
to the segment
s^{\prime}
of
\gamma^{\prime}
, while keeping
\gamma-(u^{\prime}\cup s\cup u)
fixed. By eventually reparametrizing
\gamma^{\prime}
(this corresponds to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
), assume that
\gamma^{\prime}([a,b])=s^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\gamma\equiv\gamma^{\prime}
on
[0,a]\cup[b,1]
and the rectangle paths (starting from
R_{0}
) associated to
\gamma_{|[0,a]}\equiv\gamma^{\prime}_{|[0,a]}
are identical. Let
R_{m}
be the last rectangle in the previous rectangle paths. By eventually changing our initial choice of
c_{i}
(see our discussion prior to
Initializing the induction
), therefore also the function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}
, we can assume that
R_{m}=R_{l}
. It suffices to show that the rectangle paths (starting from
R_{l}
) associated to
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
are homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.

Since
\gamma([a,b])=u^{\prime}\cup s\cup u\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
,
\gamma^{\prime}([a,b])=s^{\prime}\subset R_{l}
and by hypothesis
\gamma^{\prime}([b,1])\equiv\gamma([b,1])
remain in

\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
until they exit this region in order to enter a crossing predecessor (or successor) of
R_{k}
, we have that there exists
R\notin A
a predecessor (resp. successor) of
R_{k}
and
W
a rectangle path such that the rectangle paths (starting from
R_{l}
) associated to
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
and
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
are of the form
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k},R,W
and
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k},R,W
respectively, where
w^{\prime}_{A},w_{A}
are words formed by rectangles in
A
. Notice that the previous two rectangle paths end in the same way since
\gamma\equiv\gamma^{\prime}
on
[b,1]
. We conclude by remarking that
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
and
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k}
are homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
, thanks to Lemma
7.26
.

(b)

Assume finally that
\gamma([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
. In this case, we will show directly that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to a trivial or monotonous rectangle path.

Consider
\gamma^{\prime}
the curve obtained by pushing
\gamma
along
D
so that
s
be identified with
s^{\prime}
. The curve
\gamma^{\prime}
is simple, good, closed and of strictly smaller length than
\gamma
. By eventually reparametrizing
\gamma^{\prime}
, assume once again that
\gamma\equiv\gamma^{\prime}
on
[0,a]\cup[b,1]
; hence the rectangle paths (starting from
R_{0}
) associated to
\gamma_{|[0,a]}\equiv\gamma^{\prime}_{|[0,a]}
are identical. As before, we will assume that the last rectangle of the previous rectangle paths is
R_{l}
. Since
\gamma([a,1]),\gamma^{\prime}([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
, there exist
R_{k^{\prime}},R_{k}\in A
and
w_{A},w^{\prime}_{A}
words formed by rectangles in
A
such that the rectangle paths (starting from
R_{l}
) associated to
\gamma^{\prime}_{|[a,1]}
and
\gamma_{|[a,1]}
are respectively of the form
R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k^{\prime}}
and
R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
. Notice that
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{k^{\prime}}}\neq\emptyset
, since
\gamma(1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1)
.

Assume without any loss of generality that
R_{k^{\prime}}
is a predecessor of some generation of
R_{0}
(recall that
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)\in\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}
) and apply the induction hypothesis for
\gamma^{\prime}
, which verifies
(M(\gamma^{\prime}),n(\gamma^{\prime}))<(M(\gamma),n(\gamma))
. We have that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to the decreasing path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}}
. Consider the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
, which is of the form:

R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

By Lemma
7.26
, the previous rectangle path is homotopic by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
to the rectangle path

R_{0},R_{1},...,R_{l},w^{\prime}_{A},R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

where
w^{\prime-1}_{A}
is the rectangle path
w_{A}^{\prime}
followed from its end to its beginning. The first part of this rectangle path is by hypothesis homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the previous rectangle path is homotopic to

R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}

Recall that the second part of this rectangle path consists only of rectangles in
A
and that
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}
intersects
\overset{\circ}{R_{k^{\prime}}}
. If
R_{k}=R_{k^{\prime}}
, then by Lemma
7.26
we have that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the decreasing path
R_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k}
, which gives us the desired result. If
R_{k}
is a predecessor of some generation of
R_{k^{\prime}}
, then by Lemma
7.26
,
R_{k^{\prime}},w^{\prime-1}_{A},R_{l},w_{A},R_{k}
is homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
. We deduce that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the decreasing rectangle path
R_{0}=r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
, which gives us the desired result. Finally, assume that
R_{k}
is a successor of some generation of
R_{k^{\prime}}
. By the same argument the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to the concatenation of a decreasing (from
0
to
p
) and an increasing (from
p
to
(p+s)
) rectangle path
R_{0},r^{\prime}_{1},...,r^{\prime}_{p}=R_{k^{\prime}},r^{\prime}_{p+1},...,r^{\prime}_{p+s}=R_{k}
. However, since
\overset{\circ}{R_{k}}\cap\overset{\circ}{R_{0}}\neq\emptyset
, we get that
r^{\prime}_{p+1}=r^{\prime}_{p-1}
. Therefore, by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
, we can decrease the length of the previous path and keep doing this until we obtain an increasing, decreasing or trivial rectangle path, which gives us the desired result.

Induction step in the case where no tangency with strong
(\star)
property exists

Assume that the domains of all (stable and unstable) tangencies with property
(\star)
contain
\gamma(0)
.

Consider
s
a tangency with property
(\star)
and
D
its domain (its existence is ensured by Lemma
7.24
). We will assume without any loss of generality that
s
is a stable tangecny. Let us denote once again by
U^{\prime}
(resp.
U
) the unstable segment of
\gamma
before (resp. after)
s
, by
s^{\prime}
the stable side of
D
that is not
s
,
u^{\prime}:=U^{\prime}\cap D
and
u:=U\cap D
. We are thus -up to a change of orientation and up to interchanging
u^{\prime}
and
u
- in the case of Figure
17
. Recall that by definition of property
(\star)
,
\gamma(0)\notin s
, therefore
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap U^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap U
.

Lemma 7.27
.

Let
R_{0}
and
R_{n}
be the first and last rectangles of the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
. If
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}
(resp.
\gamma(0)\in U
),
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(U^{\prime})
(resp.
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(U)
) is trivial and
U^{\prime}\subset R_{n}
(resp.
U\subset R_{0}
), then
\gamma
is homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
to a simple, closed and good polygonal curve having at least one tangency with strong property
(\star)
.

Proof.

Indeed, by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
we can reparametrize
\gamma
so that
\gamma(0)\in s
. We obtain the desired result by Lemma
7.25
.
∎

We are now ready to describe the induction step in this final case.

(1
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(0,0,0)
, by our argument in case
(1^{\prime})
we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
D
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.27
a reparametrization of
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(2
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(1,0,1)
, we are in the case of Figure
18
b, with
u^{\prime}=U^{\prime}
and therefore
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}
. By our argument in case (2
′
), we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
D
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. By doing so
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
becomes trivial and therefore by Lemma
7.27
up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(3
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(0,0,1)
, we are in the case of Figure
18
b, where
N=0
. We apply the same argument as in case
(2^{\prime\prime})
.

(4
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(1,1,0)
, then we are in the case of Figure
19
, where
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
,
\{R_{N},R_{N+1},...,R_{m}\}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(s)
,
u=U
and
l<N<m
. By the same arguments as before, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(5
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(0,1,0)
, then either
\gamma(0)\in u\cap s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
. In both cases, by the same arguments as before, up to a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(6
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(1,1,1)
, then either
\gamma(0)\in u\cap s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
. In both cases, we can apply in the exact same way the homotopy performed in (6
′
).

(7
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(0,1,1)
, as in the case (
7^{\prime}
):

•

If
U^{\prime}\not\subset\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(x_{su})
, we are in the case of Figure
20
and we have that
\gamma(0)\in u\cap s^{\prime}
or
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
. In both cases, we can apply in the exact same way the homotopy performed in (7
′
).

•

If
U^{\prime}\subset\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(x_{su})
, then we are in the case of Figure
21
. In this case,
u^{\prime}=U^{\prime}
and
\gamma(0)\in u^{\prime}
. Once again, we can push
\gamma
by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
along
D
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
; hence by Lemma
7.27
, a reparametrization of
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We deduce the induction step by applying cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(8
′′
)

If
(U^{\prime},s,U)
is associated to
(1,0,0)
, we are in the case of case of Figure
18
a, where
\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,R_{0}}(u^{\prime})
and
N>l
. Let
A=\{R_{l},...,R_{N}\}
and
a,b\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma([a,b])=u^{\prime}\cup s\cup u
. Assume first that
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
.

(a)

As in case (
8^{\prime}
), if
\gamma_{|[b,1]}
eventually exits
\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
in order to visit a crossing predecessor (or successor) of
R_{k}\in A
. By our argument in case (8
′
a) we can push
\gamma
along
D
by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
so that
s
comes arbitrarily close to
s^{\prime}
. Hence, by Lemma
7.27
up to a homotopy,
\gamma
has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. We obtain the induction step by applying the cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
.

(b)

If now
\gamma([a,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
, since
\gamma(0)\in U^{\prime}\cap s^{\prime}
, we get that
\gamma([0,1])\subset\underset{i=l}{\overset{N}{\cup}}R_{i}
. Therefore, the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is a word formed by rectangles in
A
. The previous rectangle path is homotopic to an increasing or decreasing or trivial rectangle path, thanks to Lemma
7.26
.

(c)

Assume now that
\gamma(0)\in s^{\prime}\cap u
. If
U^{\prime}\subset D
then we can apply the exact same argument as in case
(8^{\prime}b)
.

If not, push
\gamma
along
D
so that
s
comes very close to
s^{\prime}
. By Lemma
7.27
, by reparametrizing this new curve we can assume that it has a tangency with strong property
(\star)
. Denote the reparametrized curve by
\gamma^{\prime}
. By applying our algorithm (cases
(1^{\prime})-(8^{\prime})
),
\gamma^{\prime}
is homotopic to a simple, good, closed polygonal curve
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of strictly smaller length. By the induction hypothesis the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial path. Hence, the same applies to the rectangle path associated to
\gamma^{\prime}
. By the exact same arguments as in case,
(8^{\prime}b)
we can now conclude that the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
is homotopic to a monotonous or trivial rectangle path.

This finishes the description of our induction step and the proof of Proposition
7.22
.

Proposition
7.22
implies Theorem
7.21

Consider
r_{0},...,r_{n}
a closed rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and
\gamma
a closed and good polygonal curve associated to the previous rectangle path.

By our definition of good polygonal curve (see Definition
5.2
), we get that
\gamma
has only a finite number, say
M
, of self-intersections that are also transverse. We will show the desired result by induction on
M
. If
M=0
, then we obtain the desired result from Proposition
7.22
. Suppose now that
M>0
and that the result stands for all
n\in\llbracket 0,M-1\rrbracket
.

By Remark
5.8
, there exists
0=c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}=1
a
(n+2)
-uple such that
\gamma(c_{i},c_{i+1})\subset r_{i}
and a function
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}:[0,1]\rightarrow\{r_{0},...,r_{n}\}
associated to
c_{0}<c_{1}<...c_{n+1}
sending points of
\gamma
to rectangles. We will assume that we have chosen the
c_{i}
as in Remark
5.9
.

Consider
x_{0}\in[0,1]
the biggest element in
[0,1]
for which
\gamma_{|[0,x_{0})}
is injective. Take
y\in[0,x_{0})
such that
\gamma(y)=\gamma(x_{0})
. Let
r_{k}=\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}(y)
,
R_{|[y,x_{0}]}
(resp.
R_{|[0,y]}
) be the rectangle path starting from
r_{k}
(resp.
r_{0}
) associated to the simple and
good polygonal curve
\gamma_{|[y,x_{0}]}:[y,x_{0}]\rightarrow\gamma([y,x_{0}])
(resp.
\gamma_{|[0,y]}
). By an eventual change of our choice of
c_{i}
, we may assume that
R_{|[y,x_{0}]}
and
R_{|[0,y]}
coincide with
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}([y,x_{0}])
and
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}([0,y])
respectfully.

Since
\gamma_{|[y,x_{0}]}
is simple and closed, by Proposition
7.22
its associated rectangle path
\textit{Rect}_{\gamma,r_{0}}([y,x_{0}])=\{r_{k},...,r_{l}\}
is homotopic to an increasing, decreasing or trivial rectangle path,
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
. Similarly, consider
\gamma^{\prime}
the good, closed polygonal curve formed by
\gamma([x_{0},1])
followed by
\gamma([0,y])
. Notice that
\gamma^{\prime}
has at most
M-1
self-intersections and therefore by our induction hypothesis its associated rectangle path (starting from
r_{l}
)
r_{l},...,r_{n}=r_{0},...,r_{k}
is homotopic to an increasing, decreasing or trivial rectangle path
r_{l}=R^{\prime}_{0},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}
.

Consider now the rectangle path associated to
\gamma
:
r_{0},...,r_{n}
. By a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
the previous rectangle path is homotopic to

r_{0},..r_{k},...r_{l},..,r_{n}=r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{k-1},r_{k},r_{k-1},...,r_{0}

By our previous discussion the above rectangle path is homotopic to

(7.1)

r_{0},..r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}=R_{0}^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k},r_{k-1},...,r_{0}

If
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
is the trivial rectangle path, then
r_{k}=r_{l}
and
r_{l}=R^{\prime}_{0},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}
is also the trivial rectangle path. In this case,
r_{0},...,r_{n}
is homotopic to the rectangle path
r_{0},...,r_{k-1},r_{k},r_{k-1}...,r_{0}
, which is homotopic to a trivial rectangle path by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
.

Assume now without any loss of generality that
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
is a decreasing rectangle path; hence
r_{l}
is a predecessor of some generation of
r_{k}
. We deduce that
r_{l}=R_{0}^{\prime},R^{\prime}_{1}...,R^{\prime}_{m}=r_{k}
is an increasing rectangle path. Furthermore, since the predecessors (resp. successors) of any rectangle have disjoint interiors, we have that there exists a unique decreasing (resp. increasing) rectangle path from
r_{k}
to
r_{l}
(resp. from
r_{l}
to
r_{k}
). We deduce that the rectangle paths
r_{k}=R_{0},R_{1}...,R_{s}=r_{l}
and
r_{k}=R_{m}^{\prime},R_{m-1}^{\prime}...,R^{\prime}_{1},R^{\prime}_{0}=r_{l}
are exactly the same. Therefore, by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
the rectangle path
7.1
is homotopic to:

r_{0},...r_{k},r_{k-1},..,r_{0}

Finally, the above rectangle path is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path by a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{B}
; we thus get
the desired result.

7.5.
Proof of Theorem
7.9

By following the notations introduced in the beginning of Section
7
, it suffices to prove that two centered and homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to centered and homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Indeed, let
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{n}
be a closed and centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. By Theorem
7.21
, the previous rectangle path is homotopic to the trivial rectangle path
r_{0}^{1}
. If homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, then
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
is homotopic to
r^{2}_{0}
. By our discussion, prior to Theorem
7.21
, this implies that
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{n}
is closed, which gives us the desired result. Let us now show that homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopic rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Since any homotopy of rectangle paths can be described as a sequence of homotopies of type
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
, it suffices to show that homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{C}
) in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Indeed, consider
R_{1},R_{2}
two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{A}
. The result is trivially true for homotopies of type
\mathcal{A}
.

Consider now
R_{1},R_{2}
two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
. By Definition
7.12
, the paths
R_{1},R_{2}
are of the form
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+1},r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+2},...r^{1}_{n}
and
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},r^{1}_{k+2},...,r^{1}_{n}
. It is not difficult to see that
R_{1},R_{2}
correspond in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to two rectangle paths of the form
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+1},r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+2},...r^{2}_{n}
and
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{k+2},...,r^{2}_{n}
. The previous two rectangle paths are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{B}
.

Finally, consider
R_{1},R_{2}
two centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that are homotopic by a homotopy of type
C
. Following the notations of Definition
7.19
, we may assume that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are respectively of the form:

{r^{1}}_{0},...,{r^{1}}_{k},{L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{01},...,{L^{1}}_{0s(0)},{L^{1}}_{1},{L^{1}}_{11},...,{L^{1}}_{1s(1)},...,{L^{1}}_{k},{r^{1}}_{l},{r^{1}}_{l+1},...,{r^{1}}_{n}

{r^{1}}_{0},...,{r^{1}}_{k},{L^{1}}_{0},{L^{\prime 1}}_{01},...,{L^{\prime 1}}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},{L^{1}}_{3},{L^{\prime 1}}_{31},...,{L^{\prime 1}}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,{L^{1}}_{k},{r^{1}}_{l},{r^{1}}_{l+1},...,{r^{1}}_{n}

where
({L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{1},{L^{1}}_{2},{L^{1}}_{3},{L^{1}}_{4})
and
({L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{3},{L^{1}}_{2},{L^{1}}_{1},{L^{1}}_{4})
are the two cycles starting from
L^{1}_{0}
around a boundary arc point
p^{1}\in L^{1}_{0}-\{\text{corners of $L^{1}_{0}$}\}
,
k\in\llbracket 1,4\rrbracket
,
{L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{01},...

,{L^{1}}_{0s(0)},{L^{1}}_{1},{L^{1}}_{11},

...,{L^{1}}_{1s(1)},...,{L^{1}}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
({L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{1},...,{L^{1}}_{k})
and
{L^{1}}_{0},{L^{\prime 1}}_{01},...,{L^{\prime 1}}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},{L^{1}}_{3},{L^{\prime 1}}_{31},...,{L^{\prime 1}}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,{L^{1}}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
({L^{1}}_{0},{L^{1}}_{3},...,{L^{1}}_{k})
.

Assume that
r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0}
is the rectangle path
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
associated to
r^{1}_{0},...,r^{1}_{k},L^{1}_{0}
. Let us show now that the cycle
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{4})
corresponds to a cycle of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
starting from
L^{2}_{0}
. Indeed, assume without any loss of generality that
L_{0}^{1}
contains a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p^{1}
and that
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{4})
is a positive cycle (see Figure
7.14
). Let
s^{1}_{0}
be the uppermost stable boundary component of
L_{0}^{1}
. Notice that
p^{1}\in s^{1}_{0}
. By our definition of a cycle (see Definition
7.13
),
L_{1}^{1}
corresponds to the
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
containing
p
in its leftmost unstable boundary. Assume now that
L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{01},...,L^{1}_{0s(0)},L^{1}_{1}
corresponds to
L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s(0)},L^{2}_{1}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. By the above,
L^{2}_{1}
corresponds to a
s^{2}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{2}
, where
s^{2}_{0}
is the uppermost stable boundary component of
L_{0}^{2}
. Furthermore, since
L_{1}^{1}
is not the leftmost
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
(
L^{1}_{3}
is an
s^{1}_{0}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}^{1}
at the left of
L_{1}^{1}
, see Figure
7.14
), neither is
L_{1}^{2}
. We conclude that
p^{1}
corresponds to the unique point of intersection, say
p^{2}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, of
s^{2}_{0}
with the leftmost unstable boundary component of
L_{1}^{2}
. Notice that
p^{2}
is not a corner point of
L_{0}^{2}
and
L_{0}^{2}
contains a germ of the
(-,-)
and
(+,-)
quadrants of
p^{2}
.

Similarly,
L^{1}_{2}
is associated in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to a crossing successor of
L_{1}^{2}
, say
L_{2}^{2}
, such that
p^{2}\in\partial L_{1}^{2}\cap\partial L_{2}^{2}
and
\overset{\circ}{L_{0}^{2}}\cap\overset{\circ}{L_{2}^{2}}=\emptyset
. By repeating the previous arguments, we can prove that the positive cycle
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{4})
is associated in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to the positive cycle of
p^{2}
starting from
L^{2}_{0}
. Similarly,
(L^{1}_{0},L^{1}_{3},L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{4})
is associated in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
to the negative cycle of
p^{2}
starting from
L_{0}^{2}
.

We conclude that the rectangle paths
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are respectively associated to two rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
of the form:

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s(0)},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{11},...,L^{2}_{1s(1)},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

r^{2}_{0},...,r^{2}_{k},L^{2}_{0},L^{\prime 2}_{01},...,L^{\prime 2}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},L^{2}_{3},L^{\prime 2}_{31},...,L^{\prime 2}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,L^{2}_{k},r^{2}_{l},r^{2}_{l+1},...,r^{2}_{n}

where
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{2},L^{2}_{3},L^{2}_{4})
and
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3},L^{2}_{2},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{4})
are the two cycles starting from
L^{2}_{0}
around
p^{2}\in L^{2}_{0}
. Also,
L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{01},...,L^{2}_{0s(0)},L^{2}_{1},L^{2}_{11},...,L^{2}_{1s(1)},...,L^{2}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{1},...,L^{2}_{k})
and
L^{2}_{0},L^{\prime 2}_{01},...,L^{\prime 2}_{0s^{\prime}(0)},L^{2}_{3},L^{\prime 2}_{31},

...,L^{\prime 2}_{3s^{\prime}(3)},...,L^{2}_{k}
is the rectangle path associated to the generalized rectangle path
(L^{2}_{0},L^{2}_{3},...,L^{2}_{k})
. By definition, the previous rectangle paths are homotopic by a homotopy of type
\mathcal{C}
, which gives us the desired result.

8.
Proof of Theorem B

Let
(M_{1},\Phi_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
be two transitive Anosov flows,
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
their bifoliated planes,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{u}
the stable and unstable foliations in
\mathcal{P}_{1,2}
. Assume that
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}
carry two Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
associated canonically by Theorem
3.17
to the same class of equivalent geometric types. By choosing in an appropriate way orientations on
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{s}
,
\mathcal{F}_{1,2}^{u}
and also representatives of every rectangle orbit, thanks to Lemma
3.19
, we may assume that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are canonically associated to the same geometric type
(R_{1},...,R_{n},(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
.

Let us denote
\Gamma_{1,2}
(resp.
(\Gamma_{1,2})_{M_{1,2}}
) the boundary periodic orbits associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
in
\mathcal{P}_{1,2}
(resp. in
(M_{1,2},\Phi_{1,2})
),
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
up to surgeries on
\Gamma_{1,2}
,
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1,2}}
their stable and unstable singular foliations,
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1,2}}
the lifts of
\mathcal{R}_{1,2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
,
\overline{\Gamma_{1,2}}
the lifts of
\Gamma_{1,2}
on
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
and
r_{0}^{1,2}
the origin rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
.

In Section
5
, we established that rectangle paths allow us to navigate inside the bifoliated plane, thus endowing the bifoliated plane with a “system of coordinates”. According to Theorem
7.7
, any centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
can be naturally associated to a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. In the previous section, we showed that this correspondence gives birth to a compatible system of coordinates in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1,2}}
: closed and centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
correspond to closed and centered rectangle paths in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We would like now to show Theorem B. In order to do so, by Theorem
6.10
it suffices to show
that there exists a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
and an isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
such that:

•

the image by
h
of any stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
is a stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}

•

for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(g(x))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

We will split the proof of the previous statement in two parts:

(1)

the construction of an isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})

(2)

the construction of a homeomoprhism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
with the desired properties

Let us begin with the construction of
\alpha
:

Proposition 8.1
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem B, the groups
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
are isomorphic.

Proof.

Consider
T(r_{0}^{1})\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
the set of all the rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
that have the same type as
r_{0}^{1}
. By enumerating the set of these rectangles by
\mathbb{Z}
we obtain a homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
to the group of automorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
,
Aut(\mathbb{Z})
.

The previous homomorphism is injective. Indeed, consider
g
an element in
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
that fixes one rectangle
R
in
T(r_{0}^{1})
. This implies that
g
has a fixed point in
R
and therefore it acts as an expansion or contraction on its stable leaf, which contradicts the fact that
g(R)=R
.

Consider now
T(r_{0}^{2})\subset\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
the set of all the rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
that have the same type as
r_{0}^{2}
. Let us choose an enumeration of the elements of
T(r_{0}^{2})
. Take
R\in T(r_{0}^{2})
and any centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
ending in
R
(such a rectangle path exists thanks to Corollary
5.6.1
). This rectangle path corresponds to a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
ending at
R^{\prime}\in T(r_{0}^{1})
. Notice that
R^{\prime}
does not depend on the choice of rectangle path thanks to Theorem
7.8
. We can therefore associate
R
to the same element of
\mathbb{Z}
as
R^{\prime}
. This defines an enumeration of the elements of
T(r_{0}^{2})
and once again we have an injective homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
to
Aut(\mathbb{Z})
. Therefore,
\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
and
\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
are subgroups of
Aut(\mathbb{Z})
. It suffices to show that they correspond to the exact same subgroup.

Consider
g^{1}\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})\leq Aut(\mathbb{Z})
the element sending
r_{0}^{1}
to
L^{1}\in T(r_{0}^{1})
(notice that there is a unique element doing this by our previous arguments) and
g^{2}\in\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})\leq Aut(\mathbb{Z})
the element sending
r_{0}^{2}
to
L^{2}\in T(r_{0}^{2})
, where
L^{2}
is associated to the same element of
\mathbb{Z}
as
L^{1}
. We would like to show that for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
g^{1}(k)=g^{2}(k)
.

Take
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and its corresponding rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}},\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
, say
R^{1},R^{2}
respectively. Consider
r_{0}^{1},r_{1}^{1},...,L^{1}
a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
starting from
r_{0}^{1}
and ending at
L^{1}
and its corresponding rectangle path
r_{0}^{2},r_{1}^{2},...,L^{2}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
(notice that the latter path, by definition of
L^{1}
and
L^{2}
, must end at
L^{2}
). Consider also
r_{0}^{1},{r^{\prime}_{1}}^{1},...,R^{1}
a rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
starting from
r_{0}^{1}
and ending at
R^{1}
and its corresponding rectangle path
r_{0}^{2},{r^{\prime}_{1}}^{2},...,R^{2}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
(notice once again that by the definition of
R^{1},R^{2}
the latter path must end at
R^{2}
).

It is now not difficult to see that the rectangle path
r_{0}^{1},r_{1}^{1},...,L^{1}=g^{1}(r_{0}^{1}),g^{1}({r^{\prime}_{1}}^{1}),...,g^{1}(R^{1})
corresponds to
r_{0}^{2},r_{1}^{2},...,L^{2}=g^{2}(r_{0}^{2}),g^{2}({r^{\prime}_{1}}^{2}),...,g^{2}(R^{2})
. Therefore,
g^{1}(R^{1})
and
g^{2}(R^{2})
are associated to the same element in
\mathbb{Z}
, which gives us the desired result.
∎

We are now ready to proceed to the construction of the homemorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We are first going to define
h
as a map from
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
and then we are going to show that this map extends to a homeomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Indeed, let
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. Consider a centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
ending at
R
and its corresponding centered rectangle path in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
ending at
R^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
. We define
h(R)=R^{\prime}
. Notice that
R^{\prime}
does not depend on our initial choice of rectangle path, thanks to Theorem
7.8
. Also, if we denote by
\alpha
the isomorphism given by the previous proposition, by our previous proof, we have that for any
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
:

(8.1)

h(g(R))=\alpha(g)(h(R))

Finally, by applying the same arguments for rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
we can construct a right and left inverse for
h
, therefore
h:\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
is a bijection that is equivarient with respect to the actions of the fundemental groups.

Theorem B follows from the next proposition:

Proposition 8.2
.

The previous map
h
extends to a unique homeomorphism from
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that:

•

the image by
h
of any stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
is a stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}

•

for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(g(x))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

Proof.

Let
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Recall that a quadrant of
x
is the closure of a connected component of
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
minus the union of stable and unstable leaves crossing
x
. For any quadrant
Q
of
x
, there exists a unique bi-infinite sequence
...R^{Q}_{-1},R^{Q}_{0},R^{Q}_{1},...
of rectangles in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
such that:

•

for every
i
,
R^{Q}_{i+1}
is a predecessor of
R^{Q}_{i}

•

for every
i
,
R^{Q}_{i}
contains a neighbourhood of
x
in
Q

Indeed, by the definition of a Markovian family there exists one rectangle
R^{Q}_{0}
containing a neighbourhood of
x
in the quadrant
Q
. By Lemma
3.12
, there exists a unique predecessor and successor of
R^{Q}_{0}
containing a neighbourhood of
x
in
Q
and by Lemma
3.15
any other rectangle with this property is a predecessor or a successor of some generation of
R^{Q}_{0}
.

By Lemmas
3.9
and
3.10
, we have that
\{x\}=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\cap}}R^{Q}_{k}
. We therefore define
h(x):=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\cap}}h(R^{Q}_{k})\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. Notice that for every
k
,
h(R^{Q}_{k})
is a predecessor of
h(R^{Q}_{k-1})
, therefore once again
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\cap}}h(R^{Q}_{k})
corresponds to a unique point in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We would now like to show that this point does not depend on the initial choice of quadrant for
x
.

Independence from the choice of quadrant

Assume first that
x
is not a boundary periodic point and that the previous quadrant
Q
was the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
. Consider the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
of
x
and

i:=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{Z}|\forall j\geq k\penalty\ R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{j}\text{ contains a germ of the $(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})$ and $(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})$ quadrants of $x$ }\}

Let us first notice that such an
i
belongs in
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
. Indeed, suppose that
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
, but not of the
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant of
x
. This implies that
x
belongs to a stable boundary component of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
, say
s
. By Lemmas
4.4
,
4.5
and
3.15
there exists
k_{0}
sufficiently big such that
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k_{0}}
is a
s
-crossing predecessor of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0}
, that contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
and
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrants of
x
. By the Markovian intersection property, for every
k\geq k_{0}
,
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}
also contains a germ of the
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
and
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrants of
x
. We thus get that
i\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
.

If
i=-\infty
, then the sequence associated to the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
is the exact same sequence as for
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
. This can happen for instance when
x
does not belong to the stable or unstable leaf of some boundary periodic point. Therefore, in this case we get the desired result.

If
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, then for every
j\geq i
the rectangle
R_{j}^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}
is contained in the sequence associated to the quadrant
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
. Let us denote this sequence
...,R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{-1},R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{0},...,R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1},R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i},R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i+1}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i+1},...,R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{n}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{n},...
. The rectangle
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
is the successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}=R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}
that intersects
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(x)
and that is not
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
. Assume without any loss of generality that this is the successor of
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i}
that is right above
R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
(see Figure
22
). In this case,
h
will send
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
to the successor of
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i})
that is right above
h(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
. Furthermore, since
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2}
contains
x
and a germ of its
(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})
quadrant,
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2}
is the unique successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
that intersects
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(x)
, which corresponds to the lowermost successor of
R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1}
. Therefore,
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-2})
will correspond to the lowermost successor of
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
, the successor of
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
that intersects
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(h(x))
. We can thus show thanks to Lemma
3.10
that
\overset{i-1}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
corresponds to a stable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(h(x))
crossing
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i-1})
.

Figure 22
.

We also know thanks to Lemma
3.9
that
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=i}{\cap}}h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=i}{\cap}}h(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})
corresponds to an unstable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}(h(x))
crossing
h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{i})
. We conclude that
\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{(\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\{h(x)\}
. By a similar argument we can show that
\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{(-\epsilon,-\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{(-\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k})=\{h(x)\}
, which gives us the desired result.

Assume now that
x
is a boundary periodic point. In this case, we can define
h(x)
by the same exact argument for some choice of quadrant
Q
of
x
(
x
has infinitely many quadrants in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
). Consider now
Q^{\prime}\neq Q
the quadrant of
x
that intersects
Q
along a stable leaf of
x
. By a similar argument, we can show that for every
N
,
\overset{N}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{Q^{\prime}}_{k})
corresponds to a stable segment in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}(h(x))
. Furthermore, if
t
is the generator of the stabilizer of a stable or unstable leaf of
x
, then for any quadrant
q
of
x
we have that
(t^{k}(R^{q}_{0}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a subsequence of
(R^{q}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Therefore,
\big(\alpha(t)^{k}(h(R^{q}_{0}))\big)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also a subsequence of
(h(R^{q}_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Our previous arguments imply that both
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{Q^{\prime}}_{k})
and
\overset{+\infty}{\underset{k=-\infty}{\cap}}h(R^{Q}_{k})
are fixed by
\alpha(t)
and belong to the same stable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{s}}
. They thus correspond to the same point
h(x)
, which gives us the desired result.

h(\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
and
h(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}

Indeed, assume that
x\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
h(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. There are infinitely many quadrants around
x
, therefore there exists an infinite family of rectangles
(R_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
intersecting
x
and having 2 by 2 disjoint interiors. For every
i
the rectangle
h(R_{i})
intersects
h(x)
that has only finitely many quadrants. Therefore, there exist
i\neq j
such that
\overset{\circ}{h(R_{i})}\cap\overset{\circ}{h(R_{j})}\neq\emptyset
. Therefore,
h(R_{i})
is a predecessor or successor of some generation of
h(R_{j})
. This contradicts that the fact that
h
defines a bijection from
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
to
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{2}}
that respects the relations of predecessor/successor.

By a similar argument we can show that
h(\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}})\subset\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}

h
is a homeomorphism

By applying all the above arguments for
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
, we can show that
h
admits a right and left inverse therefore
h
is a bijection.

Consider now
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}-\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
y:=h(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}-\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
. Denote by
(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
the sequence of rectangles defined in the previous paragraphs for
x
and the quadrant
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})
. The continuity of
h
on
x
follows for the fact that
(\underset{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\in\{+,-\}^{2}}{\cup}\overset{N}{\underset{k=-N}{\cap}}R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\underset{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})\in\{+,-\}^{2}}{\cup}\overset{N}{\underset{k=-N}{\cap}}h(R^{(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime})}_{k}(x)))_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
form respectively a basis of neighbourhoods of
x
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and of
y
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Consider now
x\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
,
y:=h(x)\in\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
,
\{Q_{k}(y)|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
(resp.
\{Q_{k}(x)|k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
) the set of all quadrants of
y
(resp.
x
) and
\mathcal{F}
the set of all functions from
\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Without any loss of generality we will assume that
h(Q_{k}(x))=Q_{k}(y)
. Denote by
(R^{Q_{k}(x)}_{l}(x))_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}
the sequence of rectangles defined in the previous paragraphs for
x
and the quadrant
Q_{k}(x)
. Once again the continuity of
h
on
x
follows from the fact that
(\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\cup}\overset{f(k)}{\underset{l=-f(k)}{\cap}}R^{Q_{k}(x)}_{l}(x))_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
and
(\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\cup}\overset{f(k)}{\underset{l=-f(k)}{\cap}}h(R^{Q_{k}(x)}_{l}(x)))_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
form respectively a basis of neighbourhoods of
x
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
y
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

The above prove that
h
is continuous. By applying the same arguments to
h^{-1}
, we get that
h
is a homeomorphism.

h
sends oriented stable/unstable leaves to oriented stable/unstable leaves

Consider
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
. Notice that for any choice of quadrant of
x
, say
q
, we have that
\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}(x)=\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\cup}}\overset{\infty}{\underset{l=k}{\cap}}R^{q}_{l}
Therefore,

h(\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}(x))=\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\cup}}\overset{\infty}{\underset{l=k}{\cap}}h(R^{q}_{l})=\overline{\mathcal{F}^{u}}(h(x))

In the same way, we can show that
h
sends stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
to stable leaves in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
.

Next, recall that
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
have been endowed with orientations such that the map
h
sends the predecessors of any rectangle
R\in\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
ordered from left to right, to the predecessors of
h(R)
ordered from left to right. Same for the successors of any rectangle in
\overline{\mathcal{R}_{1}}
. We deduce that
h
respects the orientations of
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}
.

h
is equivariant with respect to the actions of the fundamental groups

Consider
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
and
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
. For any quadrant
q
of
x
, we have that (see Proposition
8.1
):

h(g(x))=h(g(\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}R^{q}_{l}))=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}h(g(R^{q}_{l}))=\overset{+\infty}{\underset{l=-\infty}{\cap}}\alpha(g)(h(R^{q}_{l}))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

∎

9.
Cycles around boundary periodic points

Fix
(M,\Phi)
a transitive Anosov flow,
\mathcal{P}
its bifoliated plane endowed with an orientation,
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}
the stable/unstable foliations in
\mathcal{P}
endowed with an orientation,
\mathcal{R}
a Markovian family on
\mathcal{P}
and
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{P}
the set of boundary points of
\mathcal{R}
.

In Section
7
we introduced the notion of cycle around a boundary arc point of
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. We would like in this section to adapt this notion of cycle for boundary periodic points, in order to obtain information about the neighbourhood of these orbits in
M
. This will allow us to prove Theorems D and E.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
p
be a boundary periodic point in
\mathcal{P}
and
R\in\mathcal{R}
a rectangle containing
p
. Suppose that
p
is contained in the stable boundary component
s
of
R
. There exists no
s
-crossing predecessor of
R
containing
p
.

Proof.

Suppose that such a rectangle
L
exists. Consider
g\in\text{Stab}(p)
such that the stable boundaries of
g(R)
become very thin and the unstable ones very long. The intersection of the rectangles
g(R)
and
L
is not Markovian. Absurd.
∎

According to the previous lemma, we can not extend Definition
7.13
for boundary periodic points.

Take
p\in\mathcal{P}
a boundary periodic point and
L_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
containing
p
. Assume without any loss of generality that
L_{0}
contains a germ of the
(+,+)
quadrant of
p
(there are only a finite number of rectangles with this property up to the action of
\text{Stab}(p)
). Consider now the set
Q_{(-,+)}
of rectangles
r
intersecting a germ of the
(-,+)
quadrant of
p
and such that
L_{0}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{+}(p)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{+}(p)
. The set
Q_{(-,+)}
is not stable under the action of
\text{Stab}(p)
, but it is contained in the union of a finite number of
\text{Stab}(p)
-orbits of rectangles. We can therefore define
L_{1}
as the unique rectangle in
Q_{(-,+)}
for which for all
r\in Q_{(-,+)}

L_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{+}(p)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{+}(p)

Notice that
L_{1}
may be equal to
L_{0}
, if
L_{0}
intersects a germ of the
(+,+)
and
(-,+)
quadrants of
p
.

Next, we define
Q_{(-,-)}
, the set of rectangles
r
intersecting a germ of the
(-,-)
quadrant of
p
and such that
L_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{-}(p)\subset r\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{-}(p)
. Again, we define
L_{2}
as the unique rectangle in
Q_{(-,-)}
for which all
r\in Q_{(-,-)}

L_{2}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{-}(p)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{-}(p)

Finally, if the rectangle
L_{0}
intersects a germ of the
(+,-)
quadrant of
p
, we take
L_{3}=L_{0}
. Otherwise, we define analogously
Q_{(+,-)}
and we define
L_{3}
as the unique rectangle in
Q_{(+,-)}
for which all
r\in Q_{(+,-)}

L_{3}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{-}(p)\subseteq r\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{-}(p)

Figure 23
.
Some examples of positive pre-cycles starting from
L_{0}

By Lemma
9.1
and our previous discussion, we have that any two rectangles among
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}
have either disjoint interiors or are equal (see Figure
23
). By eventually removing a rectangle appearing twice, we can assume that the rectangles
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}
have two by two disjoint interiors.
We will call
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})

the positive pre-cycle around
p
starting from
L_{0}
. By changing the cyclic order in which we visit the quadrants around
p
, we can define similarly
the negative pre-cycle around
p
starting from
L_{0}
.

Remark 9.2
.

To every rectangle intersecting
p
we associate canonically a positive and a negative pre-cycle around
p
. The image of a pre-cycle around
p
by an element of
\text{Stab}(p)
is a pre-cycle around
p
. Therefore, there is a finite number of pre-cycles around
p
up to the action of
\text{Stab}(p)
.

Proposition 9.3
.

To any pre-cycle around
p
we can associate canonically a closed rectangle path of
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

Since a generalized rectangle path can canonically be associated to a rectangle path (see Definition
7.18
), it suffices to show that we can canonically associate any pre-cycle to a generalized rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
beginning and ending by the same rectangle. Let us consider
L_{0}
intersecting
p
and
L_{0},...,L_{k}
its associated positive pre-cycle. The case of negative pre-cycles follows from the same arguments. Assume without any loss of generality that the previous cycle is of the from
L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}
, where the
L_{i}
have two by two disjoint interiors and that
L_{0}
contains a germ of the
(+,+)
quadrant of
p
. All the other cases, follow by the same arguments.

We begin our generalized rectangle path by
L_{0}
. Let
s_{0}=L_{0}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{+}(p)
and
r_{0}
be the
s_{0}
-crossing successor of
L_{0}
containing the endpoint of
s_{0}
that is not
p
(this rectangle exists by Lemma
4.5
). By construction of
L_{1}
,
r_{0}
is a successor of
L_{1}
of some generation. Similarly, we define
s_{1}:=L_{1}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{-}(p)
,
s_{2}:=L_{2}\cap\mathcal{F}^{u}_{-}(p)
,
r_{1}
as the
s_{1}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{1}
containing the endpoint of
s_{1}
that is not
p
and
r_{2}
as the
s_{2}
-crossing successor of
L_{2}
containing the endpoint of
s_{2}
that is not
p
. Again,
r_{1}
(resp.
r_{2}
) is a predecessor (resp. successor) of
L_{2}
(resp.
L_{3}
) of some generation.

Finally, if
L_{0}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)\subset L_{3}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)
we define
s_{3}:=L_{0}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)
and
r_{3}
as the
s_{3}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{0}
intersecting the endpoint of
s_{3}
that is not
p
. If instead,
L_{3}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)\subset L_{0}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)
we define
s_{3}:=L_{3}\cap\mathcal{F}^{s}_{+}(p)
and
r_{3}
as the
s_{3}
-crossing predecessor of
L_{3}
intersecting the endpoint of
s_{3}
that is not
p
. In any case,
r_{3}
is a predecessor of some generation of
L_{0}
and also
L_{3}
.

We deduce that
(L_{0},r_{0},L_{1},r_{1},L_{2},r_{2},L_{3},r_{3},L_{0})
is a generalized rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
that is canonically associated to the pre-cycle
(L_{0},L_{1},L_{2},L_{3})
, which gives us the desired result.
∎

Definition 9.4
.

Take
p
a boundary periodic point. The set of rectangle paths associated to all pre-cycles around
p
will be called the set of
cycles
of
p
and will be denoted by
\text{Cycl}(p)
.

Remark 9.5
.

If
c
is a pre-cycle around
p
and
g\in\text{Stab}(p)
. The image by
g
of the cycle associated to
c
, is the cycle associated to the pre-cycle
g(c)
. Therefore, by Remark
9.2
, the set of cycles around any boundary periodic orbit
p
consists of a finite number of orbits by the action of
\text{Stab}(p)
.

More generally,

Remark 9.6
.

For any
g\in\pi_{1}(M)
and any boundary periodic point
p
,
g(\text{Cycl}(p))=\text{Cycl}(g(p))
. Therefore, the set of all cycles around any boundary periodic point in the
\pi_{1}(M)
-orbit of
p
, consists of a finite number of rectangle paths up to the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
.

We will now prove that each of the previous orbits of rectangle paths can be described as a closed rectangle path in any geometric type associated to
\mathcal{R}
by Theorem
3.17
.

Definition 9.7
.

Consider a geometric type
G=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
, where
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{i}^{j},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,h_{i}\rrbracket\}
and
\mathcal{V}=\{V_{i}^{j},i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 1,v_{i}\rrbracket\}
. We define a
rectangle path
in
G
as a finite sequence of the form
i_{0},s_{0},i_{1},s_{1},i_{2},...,s_{m},i_{m+1}
where

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m+1\rrbracket
,
i_{k}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
s_{k}
is of the form
V_{i_{k}}^{\bullet}
or
H_{i_{k}}^{\bullet}
.

•

for all
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, if
s_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
(resp.
s_{k}\in\mathcal{H}
) then it is the image by
\phi
(resp.
\phi^{-1}
) of an element of
\mathcal{H}
(resp.
\mathcal{V}
) of the form
H_{i_{k+1}}^{\bullet}
(resp.
V_{i_{k+1}}^{\bullet}
).

Furthermore, the rectangle path
i_{0},s_{0},i_{1},s_{1},i_{2},...,s_{m},i_{m+1}
will be called
closed
when

i_{0}=i_{m+1}

Recall that we may think of a geometric type
G
as a set of rectangles
R_{1},...,R_{n}
, endowed each with a collection of horizontal and vertical subrectangles identified by the map
(\phi,u)
. In these terms, the reader may think of a rectangle path in
G
as a sequence of the form
R_{0},s_{0},...,R_{m},s_{m},R_{m+1}
, where for every
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
,
s_{k}
corresponds to a vertical (resp. or horizontal) subrectangle of
R_{k}
and a horizontal (resp. or vertical) subrectangle of
R_{k+1}
.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
G=(n,(h_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},(v_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket},\mathcal{H},\mathcal{V},\phi,u)
be one of the geometric types associated to
\mathcal{R}
by Theorem
3.17
. Endow
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}
with orientations and choose
r_{1},...,r_{n}
representatives of every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
, so that
\mathcal{R}
is canonically associated to
G
, thanks to Lemma
3.19
. We have that:

•

any rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
corresponds to a unique rectangle path in
G

•

conversely, any rectangle path in
G
corresponds to a unique
\pi_{1}(M)
-orbit of rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}

Proof.

Consider
l_{0},l_{1},...,l_{k}
a rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}
. Once again we will think of
G
as a set of rectangles
R_{1},...,R_{n}
. By our proof of Theorem
3.17
, every
l_{i}
corresponds to a unique rectangle
R_{l_{i}}
of the geometric type
G
. Furthermore, for every
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket

l_{i}
is a successor or predecessor of
l_{i-1}
, therefore
l_{i}
corresponds to a unique horizontal or vertical subrectangle
s_{l_{i}}
of
R_{l_{i-1}}
. We therefore associate the rectangle path
l_{0},...,l_{k}
to the sequence
R_{l_{0}},s_{l_{1}},....,R_{l_{k-1}},s_{l_{k}},R_{l_{k}}
. Using our proof of Theorem
3.17
, is easy to check that the above sequence is a rectangle path in
G
.

Consider now
R_{0},s_{1},....,R_{k-1},s_{k},R_{k}
a rectangle path in
G
. By our proof of Theorem
3.17
, the rectangle
R_{0}
corresponds to a unique
\pi_{1}(M)
orbit of rectangles in
\mathcal{P}
. Let
l_{0}
be a representative of this orbit. Next,
s_{1}
corresponds to a unique successor or predecessor of
l_{0}
, say
l_{1}
and
R_{2}
to a unique predecessor or successor of
l_{1}
, say
l_{2}
. By repeating the previous procedure, we can associate
R_{0},s_{1},....,R_{k-1},s_{k},R_{k}
to the rectangle path
l_{0},...,l_{k}
in
\mathcal{P}
. The previous rectangle path depends on our choice of
l_{0}
. If we replace
l_{0}
by
g(l_{0})
, where
g\in\pi_{1}(M)
, it is easy to check that the resulting rectangle path will be the image by
g
of
l_{0},...,l_{k}
. We conclude that the rectangle path
R_{0},s_{1},....,R_{k-1},s_{k},R_{k}
is associated to a unique
\pi_{1}(M)
orbit of rectangle paths in
\mathcal{P}
.
∎

According to Theorem B, an equivalence class of geometric types describes an Anosov flow up to surgeries along boundary periodic orbits. We would like at this point to define a combinatorial object describing an Anosov flow up to just orbital equivalence.

Definition 9.9
.

Let
G
be a geometric type and
\mathcal{A}
a finite set of closed rectangle paths in
G
. We will call
(G,\mathcal{A})
a
geometric type with cycles
.

Two geometric types with cycles
(G,\mathcal{A})
and
(G^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
will be called
equivalent
if there exists an equivalence
h
between
G
and
G^{\prime}
(see Definition
2.7
) that bijectively associates closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{A}
to closed rectangle paths in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

Remark 9.10
.

Using Remark
2.8
, we can show that any equivalence class of geometric types with cycles contains only a finite number of geometric types with cycles.

By Remark
3.18
, for each choice of orientations on
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}
and each choice of representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}
, we can associate
\mathcal{R}
to a unique geometric type
G
. By Remark
2.8
, the set of geometric types obtained in this way is finite.

Fix one of the previous geometric types
G
(together with the choice of representatives and orientations associated to it). Associate now every boundary periodic point
p
of
\mathcal{R}
to the set of closed rectangle paths
\text{Cycl}(p)
(this association is canonical). By Proposition
4.2
and Remark
9.6
, the union of all the above cycles corresponds to finitely many closed rectangle paths up to the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
. Hence, by Proposition
9.8
, we get that we can canonically associate to
\mathcal{R}
a finite set
\mathcal{A}
of closed rectangle paths in
G
. We will call
(G,\mathcal{A})
the geometric type with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}
(for this choice of orientations and representatives).

By repeating our proof of Theorem
3.17
, it is now not hard to show

Theorem D

Let
M
be an orientable, closed and connected 3-manifold and
\Phi
a transitive
Anosov flow on
M
. To any Markovian family
\mathcal{R}
of
\Phi
we can associate canonically a unique and finite class of equivalent geometric types with cycles.

We would like now to prove Theorem E according to which an equivalence clas of geometric types with cycles describes the flow up to orbital equivalence. More precisely,

Theorem E

Let
(M_{1},\Phi_{1}),(M_{2},\Phi_{2})
be two transitive Anosov flows,
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
two Markovian families in
\mathcal{P}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{2}
associated to the same equivalence class of geometric types with cycles. Then
\Phi_{1}
is orbitally equivalent to
\Phi_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
be the boundary periodic points of
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
and
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
,
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
the bifoliated planes of
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
up to surgeries along
\Gamma_{1}
,
\Gamma_{2}
. Using Lemma
3.19
, it is not hard to show that by choosing properly orientations on
\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{1},\mathcal{F}^{s,u}_{2}
and representatives for every rectangle orbit in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, we may assume that the Markovian families
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
are associated to the same geometric type with cycles. By Theorem B and Theorem
6.10
, there exist an isomorphism
\alpha:\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M_{2}-\Gamma_{2})
and a homeomorphism
h:\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
such that:

•

the image by
h
of any oriented stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{1}^{s,u}}
is an oriented stable/unstable leaf in
\overline{\mathcal{F}_{2}^{s,u}}

•

for every
g\in\pi_{1}(M_{1}-\Gamma_{1})
and every
x\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
we have

h(g(x))=\alpha(g)(h(x))

Let us now go back to our proof of Theorem
6.10
and show that under the additional hypothesis on the cycles of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, the flows
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
are orbitally equivalent.

Indeed, in the proof of Theorem
6.10
, for every periodic point
\overline{\gamma}
in
\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
(resp.
\overline{\Gamma_{2}}
) we defined a canonical basis
s^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}},t^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
s^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}},t^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
) of its stabilizer group, where
s^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
s^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
) fixes all the stable (or unstable) leaves intersecting
\overline{\gamma}
and
t^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
(resp.
t^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
) acts as a translation by either
+1
or
+2
on the previous set depending on whether
\overline{\gamma}
has respectfully negative or positive eigenvalues. By convention, we will assume that
s^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
and
s^{M_{2}}_{\overline{\gamma}}
act as expansions on every unstable leaf crossing
\overline{\gamma}
. We also proved that for every boundary periodic orbit
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}

\alpha(s^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})=s^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}

\alpha(t^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}})=t^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}+k\cdot s^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}

where
k
belongs in
\mathbb{Z}
and only depends on the projection of the orbit
\overline{\gamma}
on
M_{1}
. We thus associated to every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
an integer
k(\gamma)
. Finally, we showed that
\Phi_{2}
is orbitally equivalent to the flow obtained by performing a surgery on
\Phi_{1}
of coefficient
-k(\gamma)
along every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that for every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{1}
we have
k(\gamma)=0
.

Fix
\overline{\gamma}\in\overline{\Gamma_{1}}
and
\gamma
its projection on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Consider a positive cycle around
\gamma
, say
r_{0},...,r_{k}=r_{0}
. Lift the previous cycle to a rectangle path
\overline{r_{0}},...,\overline{r_{k}}
in
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{1}}
intersecting
\overline{\gamma}
. By the construction of the canonical geometric type with cycles associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\mathcal{R}_{2}
(for our choice of orientations and rectangle representatives), every cycle in
\mathcal{P}_{1}
corresponds to a cycle of the geometric type associated to
\mathcal{R}_{1}
and
\mathcal{R}_{2}
; therefore it also corresponds to a cycle in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. Therefore,
h(\overline{r_{0}}),...,h(\overline{r_{k}})
projects to a closed rectangle path in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Since
r_{0},...,r_{k}=r_{0}
goes once around
\gamma
, if
\gamma
has positive (resp. negative) eigenvalues,
t^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}(\overline{r_{0}})=\overline{r_{k}}
(resp.
2\cdot t^{M_{1}}_{\overline{\gamma}}(\overline{r_{0}})=\overline{r_{k}}
). Thus, by applying
h
, if
\gamma
has positive (resp. negative) eigenvalues,
[t^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}+k(\gamma)\cdot s^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}](h(\overline{r_{0}}))=h(\overline{r_{k}})

(
resp.
[2\cdot t^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}+2k(\gamma)\cdot s^{M_{2}}_{h(\overline{\gamma})}](h(\overline{r_{0}}))=h(\overline{r_{k}})
)
. By our previous arguments,
h(\overline{r_{0}})
and
h(\overline{r_{k}})
project to the same rectangles in
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, which is only possible when
k(\gamma)=0
.
∎
